Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, Doctor, I’m here because for months now I’ve been seeing the exact same pattern. It’s... well, my sister’s appointments keep getting moved around, bills show up completely wrong, and people just conveniently "forget" to call me back. 

And speaking of calling back, I have this one girl at my office—I supervise the administrative support staff downtown—and she used to do the exact same thing. She'd say she forgot to transfer the calls, but I know she was just busy planning her wedding on company time. I told her, you know, having a bachelor's degree doesn't teach you work ethic. I got my degree years ago and we actually worked. We didn't have smartphones to distract us, just proper filing and respect. I had this beautiful oak desk back then, and my supervisor at the time, Mr. Henderson, he always said I had the sharpest eye for detail in the whole department... 

Wait, what was I saying? Oh, right. The appointments. Everyone keeps telling me it’s just mistakes, just administrative errors. But I know better. I know they’re deliberately keeping information from me and trying to push me out of decisions.

Heard or seen things? Like ghosts or something? No, Doctor, I'm not crazy. I don't see things that aren't there. My vision is perfectly fine, actually. I just had my eyes checked last month at that new optometrist over on 4th Street. Dr. Evans, I think his name was. Very young, maybe too young, honestly. He had this whole high-tech machine that puffs air into your eye—I hate that thing. When I was a girl, Dr. Miller just used a simple light and a chart. He was such a wonderful man, Dr. Miller. He used to give out these little butterscotch candies after your appointment. You know, the ones wrapped in the yellow cellophane? I haven't seen those in years. I tried looking for them at the grocery store for Halloween, but all they have now is that sour gummy nonsense. Kids today don't appreciate a good hard candy. They just want instant gratification. It's the same with the young staff at my office, like I was saying before. No patience. No attention to detail. 

But no, to answer your question, I don't hear voices or see things. I just see what's actually happening right in front of me, even if everyone else wants to pretend it's not.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Mistaken? No, Doctor, I'm not mistaken. I know what I'm seeing. Like I said when I first sat down—the appointments moving, the wrong bills, the "forgotten" phone calls... it's a pattern. Everyone keeps trying to tell me it's just administrative errors, just mistakes. But I know they're deliberately keeping information from me. They want to push me out of making decisions. 

It's just like when my husband and I bought our first house. The real estate agent, she tried to tell us the plumbing issues were just "routine maintenance." Routine! We had to rip out the entire guest bathroom. It was a nightmare. We had this lovely floral wallpaper in there, hydrangeas, I think. Or maybe peonies. I always get those two mixed up. My mother had the most beautiful peony bush in her front yard. Every spring, it would just explode with pink. She used to cut them and put them in this crystal vase she got for her wedding. Real crystal, not that cheap glass they sell now. It caught the light so beautifully in the afternoons... 

But anyway, no. I'm not mistaken about this. I know what they're doing. They can call it mistakes all they want, but I see right through it.

We had this lovely floral wallpaper in there, hydrangeas, I think. Or maybe peonies. I always get those two mixed up. My mother had the most beautiful peony bush in her front yard. Every spring, it would just explode with pink. She used to cut them and put them in this crystal vase she got for her wedding. Real crystal, not that cheap glass they sell now. It caught the light so beautifully in the afternoons... \n\nBut anyway, no. I'm not mistaken about this. I know what they're doing. They can call it mistakes all they want, but I see right through it.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been this suspicious of other people for as long as you can remember, or did this only start recently?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 59 / first_line_supervisor_of_office_or_administrative_support_worker / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Duration\nmanifestation: \"This has been going on for about 11 months, since my sister’s health suddenly got worse and I had to start coordinating her care.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Recently? Well, I suppose it depends on what you mean by recently. But no, I haven't always been like this. I used to be very trusting, probably too trusting if you ask me. But this specific situation, with the appointments and the information being hidden, that started about 11 months ago. Right around the time my sister's health took a sudden turn for the worse and I had to step in to coordinate all her care. 

She has this lovely little house over in the historic district, you know, the ones with the big wrap-around porches? We used to sit out there in the evenings with a glass of iced tea. She grew mint in these terracotta pots on the steps. Real mint, not that peppermint extract nonsense they put in everything now. It smelled so fresh, especially after a summer rain. We'd just sit and watch the fireflies. You don't see fireflies like you used to, do you? I think it's all the pesticides people spray on their lawns. Everyone wants that perfect, unnatural green grass. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he's out there every Saturday morning at 7 AM with his lawnmower and his chemicals. It's ridiculous. I told him once, I said, "You're poisoning the earth, Arthur." He just ignored me, of course. Typical. 

But yes, 11 months ago. That's when I had to start dealing with all these doctors and insurance people for her, and that's when I realized how they operate. How they try to keep you in the dark.

Right around the time my sister's health took a sudden turn for the worse and I had to step in to coordinate all her care. \n\nShe has this lovely little house over in the historic district, you know, the ones with the big wrap-around porches? We used to sit out there in the evenings with a glass of iced tea. She grew mint in these terracotta pots on the steps. Real mint, not that peppermint extract nonsense they put in everything now. It smelled so fresh, especially after a summer rain. We'd just sit and watch the fireflies. You don't see fireflies like you used to, do you? I think it's all the pesticides people spray on their lawns. Everyone wants that perfect, unnatural green grass. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he's out there every Saturday morning at 7 AM with his lawnmower and his chemicals. It's ridiculous. I told him once, I said, \"You're poisoning the earth, Arthur.\" He just ignored me, of course. Typical. \n\nBut yes, 11 months ago. That's when I had to start dealing with all these doctors and insurance people for her, and that's when I realized how they operate. How they try to keep you in the dark.\n\n[Last Question]\nBefore these last 11 months, had you generally been suspicious of other people for most of your adult life?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 59 / first_line_supervisor_of_office_or_administrative_support_worker / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, no, I wouldn't say I've always been suspicious. Like I said, I used to be quite trusting. Almost naive, really. I remember when I first started working as an administrative supervisor—oh, this must have been thirty years ago now. I was so eager to please everyone. I used to bring in homemade baked goods every Friday. Muffins, mostly. Blueberry, bran, sometimes a nice lemon poppyseed. I had this wonderful recipe for lemon poppyseed muffins from my grandmother. She used real lemon zest, not that artificial flavoring. You had to grate the lemons yourself, which took forever, but the smell... oh, it was heavenly. It would fill her whole kitchen. She had this bright yellow wallpaper in her kitchen, with little white daisies on it. Very cheerful. Very 1970s. I think my cousin still has the old mixing bowl she used to use. It was one of those heavy ceramic ones, the kind you just can't find anymore unless you go to an antique store. I love antique stores, don't you? You never know what you're going to find. Just last week, I saw the most beautiful silver tea set in the window of that shop downtown, but the parking down there is just impossible... 

But, what were we talking about? Oh, right. Being suspicious. No, before all this started with my sister's care, I just went about my business and assumed people were doing their jobs properly.

You had to grate the lemons yourself, which took forever, but the smell... oh, it was heavenly. It would fill her whole kitchen. It was one of those heavy ceramic ones, the kind you just can't find anymore unless you go to an antique store. I love antique stores, don't you? You never know what you're going to find. Being suspicious. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, not really. I mean, I'm not a paranoid person, if that's what you're getting at. I just go to work, do my job... I've been at the same company for fifteen years now. Fifteen years! You'd think they would appreciate that kind of loyalty. When I first started, the office manager was this wonderful woman named Barbara. Barbara had the most incredible collection of silk scarves. She wore a different one every day, tied in these elaborate knots. I asked her once how she did it, and she said she learned it from a magazine in the eighties. Vogue, maybe? Or Cosmopolitan. I used to read Cosmopolitan when I was in my twenties, but then it just got so... vulgar. The articles nowadays, I don't know who reads them. Young people, I suppose. My niece, she's twenty-two, and the things she posts on the internet... I don't understand it. Why would you want the whole world to know what you had for breakfast? She took a picture of an avocado toast last week. Avocado toast! It's just bread and a vegetable. Well, a fruit, technically. Avocados have large pits, so they're fruits. Like peaches. Oh, I love a good peach cobbler in the summer... 

But no, to answer your question, I don't just walk around mistrusting everyone. It's only this specific thing with my sister's care because it's so obviously happening right in front of me.

I’m still managing my responsibilities, but it has started to affect how people respond to me.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 11 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about 11 months, since my sister’s health suddenly got worse and I had to start coordinating her care.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 59 / first_line_supervisor_of_office_or_administrative_support_worker / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S019\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you been this suspicious of other people for as long as you can remember, or did this only start recently?\n\nPatient: Recently? Well, I suppose it depends on what you mean by recently. But no, I haven't always been like this. I used to be very trusting, probably too trusting if you ask me. But this specific situation, with the appointments and the information being hidden, that started about 11 months ago. Right around the time my sister's health took a sudden turn for the worse and I had to step in to coordinate all her care. \n\nShe has this lovely little house over in the historic district, you know, the ones with the big wrap-around porches? We used to sit out there in the evenings with a glass of iced tea. She grew mint in these terracotta pots on the steps. Real mint, not that peppermint extract nonsense they put in everything now. It smelled so fresh, especially after a summer rain. We'd just sit and watch the fireflies. You don't see fireflies like you used to, do you? I think it's all the pesticides people spray on their lawns. Everyone wants that perfect, unnatural green grass. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he's out there every Saturday morning at 7 AM with his lawnmower and his chemicals. It's ridiculous. I told him once, I said, \"You're poisoning the earth, Arthur.\" He just ignored me, of course. Typical. \n\nBut yes, 11 months ago. That's when I had to start dealing with all these doctors and insurance people for her, and that's when I realized how they operate. How they try to keep you in the dark.\n\nDoctor: Before these last 11 months, had you generally been suspicious of other people for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say I've always been suspicious. Like I said, I used to be quite trusting. Almost naive, really. I remember when I first started working as an administrative supervisor—oh, this must have been thirty years ago now. I was so eager to please everyone. I used to bring in homemade baked goods every Friday. Muffins, mostly. Blueberry, bran, sometimes a nice lemon poppyseed. I had this wonderful recipe for lemon poppyseed muffins from my grandmother. She used real lemon zest, not that artificial flavoring. You had to grate the lemons yourself, which took forever, but the smell... oh, it was heavenly. It would fill her whole kitchen. She had this bright yellow wallpaper in her kitchen, with little white daisies on it. Very cheerful. Very 1970s. I think my cousin still has the old mixing bowl she used to use. It was one of those heavy ceramic ones, the kind you just can't find anymore unless you go to an antique store. I love antique stores, don't you? You never know what you're going to find. Just last week, I saw the most beautiful silver tea set in the window of that shop downtown, but the parking down there is just impossible... \n\nBut, what were we talking about? Oh, right. Being suspicious. No, before all this started with my sister's care, I just went about my business and assumed people were doing their jobs properly.\n\nDoctor: Outside of this recent situation, do you tend to mistrust or suspect other people in many parts of your life?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I'm not a paranoid person, if that's what you're getting at. I just go to work, do my job... I've been at the same company for fifteen years now. Fifteen years! You'd think they would appreciate that kind of loyalty. When I first started, the office manager was this wonderful woman named Barbara. Barbara had the most incredible collection of silk scarves. She wore a different one every day, tied in these elaborate knots. I asked her once how she did it, and she said she learned it from a magazine in the eighties. Vogue, maybe? Or Cosmopolitan. I used to read Cosmopolitan when I was in my twenties, but then it just got so... vulgar. The articles nowadays, I don't know who reads them. Young people, I suppose. My niece, she's twenty-two, and the things she posts on the internet... I don't understand it. Why would you want the whole world to know what you had for breakfast? She took a picture of an avocado toast last week. Avocado toast! It's just bread and a vegetable. Well, a fruit, technically. Avocados have large pits, so they're fruits. Like peaches. Oh, I love a good peach cobbler in the summer... \n\nBut no, to answer your question, I don't just walk around mistrusting everyone. It's only this specific thing with my sister's care because it's so obviously happening right in front of me.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you also find yourself mistrusting close friends or family members, or is it mainly this situation with your sister’s care and work?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. No, no, I wouldn't say I mistrust my friends or family. Well, except maybe my cousin Elaine, but that's a whole different story. She borrowed a Tupperware container from me at Thanksgiving—the good kind, you know, the vintage ones with the starburst seal on the lid? They just don't make them like that anymore. Now it's all that flimsy plastic that warps in the dishwasher. Anyway, she swore she returned it, but I know for a fact she didn't. I've looked everywhere for it. I even checked the top shelf of the pantry, where I keep the holiday tins. I have quite a collection of those tins. Some of them are from the 1950s. There's one with a beautiful winter scene, little children ice skating on a pond... 

But, what was I saying? Oh. No, my friends and family are fine. It's really just the people handling my sister's care. That's where the real problem is.

Kids today don't appreciate a good hard candy. They just want instant gratification. It's the same with the young staff at my office, like I was saying before. No patience. No attention to detail. \n\nBut no, to answer your question, I don't hear voices or see things. I just see what's actually happening right in front of me, even if everyone else wants to pretend it's not.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there is any chance you might be mistaken about what these people are doing?\n\nPatient: Mistaken? No, Doctor, I'm not mistaken. I know what I'm seeing. Like I said when I first sat down—the appointments moving, the wrong bills, the \"forgotten\" phone calls... it's a pattern. Everyone keeps trying to tell me it's just administrative errors, just mistakes. But I know they're deliberately keeping information from me. They want to push me out of making decisions. \n\nIt's just like when my husband and I bought our first house. The real estate agent, she tried to tell us the plumbing issues were just \"routine maintenance.\" Routine! We had to rip out the entire guest bathroom. It was a nightmare. We had this lovely floral wallpaper in there, hydrangeas, I think. Or maybe peonies. I always get those two mixed up. My mother had the most beautiful peony bush in her front yard. Every spring, it would just explode with pink. She used to cut them and put them in this crystal vase she got for her wedding. Real crystal, not that cheap glass they sell now. It caught the light so beautifully in the afternoons... \n\nBut anyway, no. I'm not mistaken about this. I know what they're doing. They can call it mistakes all they want, but I see right through it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been this suspicious of other people for as long as you can remember, or did this only start recently?\n\nPatient: Recently? Well, I suppose it depends on what you mean by recently. But no, I haven't always been like this. I used to be very trusting, probably too trusting if you ask me. But this specific situation, with the appointments and the information being hidden, that started about 11 months ago. Right around the time my sister's health took a sudden turn for the worse and I had to step in to coordinate all her care. \n\nShe has this lovely little house over in the historic district, you know, the ones with the big wrap-around porches? We used to sit out there in the evenings with a glass of iced tea. She grew mint in these terracotta pots on the steps. Real mint, not that peppermint extract nonsense they put in everything now. It smelled so fresh, especially after a summer rain. We'd just sit and watch the fireflies. You don't see fireflies like you used to, do you? I think it's all the pesticides people spray on their lawns. Everyone wants that perfect, unnatural green grass. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he's out there every Saturday morning at 7 AM with his lawnmower and his chemicals. It's ridiculous. I told him once, I said, \"You're poisoning the earth, Arthur.\" He just ignored me, of course. Typical. \n\nBut yes, 11 months ago. That's when I had to start dealing with all these doctors and insurance people for her, and that's when I realized how they operate. How they try to keep you in the dark.\n\nDoctor: Before these last 11 months, had you generally been suspicious of other people for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say I've always been suspicious. Like I said, I used to be quite trusting. Almost naive, really. I remember when I first started working as an administrative supervisor—oh, this must have been thirty years ago now. I was so eager to please everyone. I used to bring in homemade baked goods every Friday. Muffins, mostly. Blueberry, bran, sometimes a nice lemon poppyseed. I had this wonderful recipe for lemon poppyseed muffins from my grandmother. She used real lemon zest, not that artificial flavoring. You had to grate the lemons yourself, which took forever, but the smell... oh, it was heavenly. It would fill her whole kitchen. She had this bright yellow wallpaper in her kitchen, with little white daisies on it. Very cheerful. Very 1970s. I think my cousin still has the old mixing bowl she used to use. It was one of those heavy ceramic ones, the kind you just can't find anymore unless you go to an antique store. I love antique stores, don't you? You never know what you're going to find. Just last week, I saw the most beautiful silver tea set in the window of that shop downtown, but the parking down there is just impossible... \n\nBut, what were we talking about? Oh, right. Being suspicious. No, before all this started with my sister's care, I just went about my business and assumed people were doing their jobs properly.\n\nDoctor: Outside of this recent situation, do you tend to mistrust or suspect other people in many parts of your life?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I'm not a paranoid person, if that's what you're getting at. I just go to work, do my job... I've been at the same company for fifteen years now. Fifteen years! You'd think they would appreciate that kind of loyalty. When I first started, the office manager was this wonderful woman named Barbara. Barbara had the most incredible collection of silk scarves. She wore a different one every day, tied in these elaborate knots. I asked her once how she did it, and she said she learned it from a magazine in the eighties. Vogue, maybe? Or Cosmopolitan. I used to read Cosmopolitan when I was in my twenties, but then it just got so... vulgar. The articles nowadays, I don't know who reads them. Young people, I suppose. My niece, she's twenty-two, and the things she posts on the internet... I don't understand it. Why would you want the whole world to know what you had for breakfast? She took a picture of an avocado toast last week. Avocado toast! It's just bread and a vegetable. Well, a fruit, technically. Avocados have large pits, so they're fruits. Like peaches. Oh, I love a good peach cobbler in the summer... \n\nBut no, to answer your question, I don't just walk around mistrusting everyone. It's only this specific thing with my sister's care because it's so obviously happening right in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you also find yourself mistrusting close friends or family members, or is it mainly this situation with your sister’s care and work?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say I mistrust my friends or family. Well, except maybe my cousin Elaine, but that's a whole different story. She borrowed a Tupperware container from me at Thanksgiving—the good kind, you know, the vintage ones with the starburst seal on the lid? They just don't make them like that anymore. Now it's all that flimsy plastic that warps in the dishwasher. Anyway, she swore she returned it, but I know for a fact she didn't. I've looked everywhere for it. I even checked the top shelf of the pantry, where I keep the holiday tins. I have quite a collection of those tins. Some of them are from the 1950s. There's one with a beautiful winter scene, little children ice skating on a pond... \n\nBut, what was I saying? Oh. No, my friends and family are fine. It's really just the people handling my sister's care. That's where the real problem is.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I feel like we keep circling back to this, Doctor. But no, I really don't. Like I said, I'm just a normal person going about my week. I go to work, I go to my Wednesday evening book club... I certainly don't sit there eyeing the other ladies suspiciously. Cape May is just gorgeous in the autumn. All those Victorian houses painted in those beautiful pastel colors. We stayed in a room that had a genuine clawfoot tub. I've always wanted a clawfoot tub in my own house, but the plumbing in our master bathroom just isn't set up for it. The contractor told us it would cost an absolute fortune to reroute the pipes, and with the price of copper these days... you wouldn't believe what they charge just for parts.

I told him once, I said, \"You're poisoning the earth, Arthur.\" He just ignored me, of course. Typical. \n\nBut yes, 11 months ago. That's when I had to start dealing with all these doctors and insurance people for her, and that's when I realized how they operate. How they try to keep you in the dark.\n\nDoctor: Before these last 11 months, had you generally been suspicious of other people for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say I've always been suspicious. Like I said, I used to be quite trusting. Almost naive, really. I remember when I first started working as an administrative supervisor—oh, this must have been thirty years ago now. I was so eager to please everyone. I used to bring in homemade baked goods every Friday. Muffins, mostly. Blueberry, bran, sometimes a nice lemon poppyseed. I had this wonderful recipe for lemon poppyseed muffins from my grandmother. She used real lemon zest, not that artificial flavoring. You had to grate the lemons yourself, which took forever, but the smell... oh, it was heavenly. It would fill her whole kitchen. She had this bright yellow wallpaper in her kitchen, with little white daisies on it. Very cheerful. Very 1970s. I think my cousin still has the old mixing bowl she used to use. It was one of those heavy ceramic ones, the kind you just can't find anymore unless you go to an antique store. I love antique stores, don't you? You never know what you're going to find. Just last week, I saw the most beautiful silver tea set in the window of that shop downtown, but the parking down there is just impossible... \n\nBut, what were we talking about? Oh, right. Being suspicious. No, before all this started with my sister's care, I just went about my business and assumed people were doing their jobs properly.\n\nDoctor: Outside of this recent situation, do you tend to mistrust or suspect other people in many parts of your life?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I'm not a paranoid person, if that's what you're getting at. I just go to work, do my job... I've been at the same company for fifteen years now. Fifteen years! You'd think they would appreciate that kind of loyalty. When I first started, the office manager was this wonderful woman named Barbara. Barbara had the most incredible collection of silk scarves. She wore a different one every day, tied in these elaborate knots. I asked her once how she did it, and she said she learned it from a magazine in the eighties. Vogue, maybe? Or Cosmopolitan. I used to read Cosmopolitan when I was in my twenties, but then it just got so... vulgar. The articles nowadays, I don't know who reads them. Young people, I suppose. My niece, she's twenty-two, and the things she posts on the internet... I don't understand it. Why would you want the whole world to know what you had for breakfast? She took a picture of an avocado toast last week. Avocado toast! It's just bread and a vegetable. Well, a fruit, technically. Avocados have large pits, so they're fruits. Like peaches. Oh, I love a good peach cobbler in the summer... \n\nBut no, to answer your question, I don't just walk around mistrusting everyone. It's only this specific thing with my sister's care because it's so obviously happening right in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you also find yourself mistrusting close friends or family members, or is it mainly this situation with your sister’s care and work?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say I mistrust my friends or family. Well, except maybe my cousin Elaine, but that's a whole different story. She borrowed a Tupperware container from me at Thanksgiving—the good kind, you know, the vintage ones with the starburst seal on the lid? They just don't make them like that anymore. Now it's all that flimsy plastic that warps in the dishwasher. Anyway, she swore she returned it, but I know for a fact she didn't. I've looked everywhere for it. I even checked the top shelf of the pantry, where I keep the holiday tins. I have quite a collection of those tins. Some of them are from the 1950s. There's one with a beautiful winter scene, little children ice skating on a pond... \n\nBut, what was I saying? Oh. No, my friends and family are fine. It's really just the people handling my sister's care. That's where the real problem is.\n\nDoctor: Outside of the situation with your sister’s care, do you find yourself suspicious of people in other parts of your life too?\n\nPatient: I feel like we keep circling back to this, Doctor. But no, I really don't. Like I said, I'm just a normal person going about my week. I go to work, I go to my Wednesday evening book club... I certainly don't sit there eyeing the other ladies suspiciously. Well, except for Brenda, but that's only because she always claims she read the book when she clearly just skimmed the Wikipedia summary. \n\nLast week we were supposed to read this wonderful historical fiction about a lighthouse keeper in the 1920s. I've always loved lighthouses. Did you know the oldest active lighthouse in the US is in New Jersey? Sandy Hook, I think it's called. My husband and I drove past it once on our way to a bed and breakfast in Cape May. Cape May is just gorgeous in the autumn. All those Victorian houses painted in those beautiful pastel colors. We stayed in a room that had a genuine clawfoot tub. I've always wanted a clawfoot tub in my own house, but the plumbing in our master bathroom just isn't set up for it. The contractor told us it would cost an absolute fortune to reroute the pipes, and with the price of copper these days... you wouldn't believe what they charge just for parts. \n\nAnyway... what was the question? Oh, right. No, I don't have a problem with people in general. I'm not a suspicious person. It's just the very real situation with my sister's care that I'm dealing with. Everything else in my life is perfectly fine.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. The contractor told us it would cost an absolute fortune to reroute the pipes, and with the price of copper these days... you wouldn't believe what they charge just for parts. \n\nAnyway... what was the question? Oh, right. No, I don't have a problem with people in general. I'm not a suspicious person. It's just the very real situation with my sister's care that I'm dealing with. Everything else in my life is perfectly fine.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you always tended to be suspicious of people and their motives, even before this recent situation started?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 59 / first_line_supervisor_of_office_or_administrative_support_worker / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I feel like you're asking me the exact same question in five different ways, Doctor. No, I haven't always been suspicious. I really don't go around looking for people's secret motives. 

Although, speaking of motives, my old neighbor Mr. Henderson always had an ulterior motive whenever he offered to sweep the shared walkway between our houses. He really just wanted an excuse to complain about my hydrangeas. Hydrangeas are incredibly temperamental, you know. You have to get the soil pH absolutely perfect. If it's too acidic, the blooms turn blue, but I've always preferred the pink ones. I tried adding garden lime to the soil a few years ago to sweeten it, but I think I used a heavy hand because the leaves got all yellow and crispy at the edges. I bought the lime at that little independent garden center on Elm Street—the one that used to be an old service station back in the seventies. You can actually still see the faint outline in the concrete where the gas pumps used to be. They sell the most wonderful heirloom tomato seedlings there in the spring, though. I usually plant a few Cherokee Purples. They make a lovely caprese salad. Just some thick slices, fresh mozzarella, a little basil from my kitchen windowsill, and a good thick balsamic glaze... 

Wait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, before all this started. No, I've always taken people at face value. I've never had a reason not to, until this business with my sister.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22.0, F60.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m Dr. __. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, Doctor, I’m here because for months now I’ve been seeing the exact same pattern. It’s... well, my sister’s appointments keep getting moved around, bills show up completely wrong, and people just conveniently \"forget\" to call me back. \n\nAnd speaking of calling back, I have this one girl at my office—I supervise the administrative support staff downtown—and she used to do the exact same thing. She'd say she forgot to transfer the calls, but I know she was just busy planning her wedding on company time. I told her, you know, having a bachelor's degree doesn't teach you work ethic. I got my degree years ago and we actually worked. We didn't have smartphones to distract us, just proper filing and respect. I had this beautiful oak desk back then, and my supervisor at the time, Mr. Henderson, he always said I had the sharpest eye for detail in the whole department... \n\nWait, what was I saying? Oh, right. The appointments. Everyone keeps telling me it’s just mistakes, just administrative errors. But I know better. I know they’re deliberately keeping information from me and trying to push me out of decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe these people are deliberately trying to harm or persecute you?\n\nPatient: Harm me? Well... I don't know if I'd say *harm*, like physical harm, but they are certainly trying to push me out, to make me look incompetent. Just like that girl at work I mentioned. It's all about making me look bad so they can take over. Which reminds me, my sister's neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she had something similar happen. She was on the HOA board, and they just slowly stopped sending her the meeting minutes. Claimed it was a \"glitch\" in the email system. Can you believe that? A glitch. I told my sister, I said, \"Martha, you watch your back around that HOA.\" Martha and I used to go to these lovely estate sales on the weekends before all this started. But anyway... what did you ask me? Oh, right, persecute. I wouldn't use that word, no. I just know they're hiding things from me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard or seen things that other people around you could not?\n\nPatient: Heard or seen things? Like ghosts or something? No, Doctor, I'm not crazy. I don't see things that aren't there. My vision is perfectly fine, actually. I just had my eyes checked last month at that new optometrist over on 4th Street. Dr. Evans, I think his name was. Very young, maybe too young, honestly. He had this whole high-tech machine that puffs air into your eye—I hate that thing. When I was a girl, Dr. Miller just used a simple light and a chart. He was such a wonderful man, Dr. Miller. He used to give out these little butterscotch candies after your appointment. You know, the ones wrapped in the yellow cellophane? I haven't seen those in years. I tried looking for them at the grocery store for Halloween, but all they have now is that sour gummy nonsense. Kids today don't appreciate a good hard candy. They just want instant gratification. It's the same with the young staff at my office, like I was saying before. No patience. No attention to detail. \n\nBut no, to answer your question, I don't hear voices or see things. I just see what's actually happening right in front of me, even if everyone else wants to pretend it's not.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there is any chance you might be mistaken about what these people are doing?\n\nPatient: Mistaken? No, Doctor, I'm not mistaken. I know what I'm seeing. Like I said when I first sat down—the appointments moving, the wrong bills, the \"forgotten\" phone calls... it's a pattern. Everyone keeps trying to tell me it's just administrative errors, just mistakes. But I know they're deliberately keeping information from me. They want to push me out of making decisions. \n\nIt's just like when my husband and I bought our first house. The real estate agent, she tried to tell us the plumbing issues were just \"routine maintenance.\" Routine! We had to rip out the entire guest bathroom. It was a nightmare. We had this lovely floral wallpaper in there, hydrangeas, I think. Or maybe peonies. I always get those two mixed up. My mother had the most beautiful peony bush in her front yard. Every spring, it would just explode with pink. She used to cut them and put them in this crystal vase she got for her wedding. Real crystal, not that cheap glass they sell now. It caught the light so beautifully in the afternoons... \n\nBut anyway, no. I'm not mistaken about this. I know what they're doing. They can call it mistakes all they want, but I see right through it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been this suspicious of other people for as long as you can remember, or did this only start recently?\n\nPatient: Recently? Well, I suppose it depends on what you mean by recently. But no, I haven't always been like this. I used to be very trusting, probably too trusting if you ask me. But this specific situation, with the appointments and the information being hidden, that started about 11 months ago. Right around the time my sister's health took a sudden turn for the worse and I had to step in to coordinate all her care. \n\nShe has this lovely little house over in the historic district, you know, the ones with the big wrap-around porches? We used to sit out there in the evenings with a glass of iced tea. She grew mint in these terracotta pots on the steps. Real mint, not that peppermint extract nonsense they put in everything now. It smelled so fresh, especially after a summer rain. We'd just sit and watch the fireflies. You don't see fireflies like you used to, do you? I think it's all the pesticides people spray on their lawns. Everyone wants that perfect, unnatural green grass. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he's out there every Saturday morning at 7 AM with his lawnmower and his chemicals. It's ridiculous. I told him once, I said, \"You're poisoning the earth, Arthur.\" He just ignored me, of course. Typical. \n\nBut yes, 11 months ago. That's when I had to start dealing with all these doctors and insurance people for her, and that's when I realized how they operate. How they try to keep you in the dark.\n\nDoctor: Before these last 11 months, had you generally been suspicious of other people for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say I've always been suspicious. Like I said, I used to be quite trusting. Almost naive, really. I remember when I first started working as an administrative supervisor—oh, this must have been thirty years ago now. I was so eager to please everyone. I used to bring in homemade baked goods every Friday. Muffins, mostly. Blueberry, bran, sometimes a nice lemon poppyseed. I had this wonderful recipe for lemon poppyseed muffins from my grandmother. She used real lemon zest, not that artificial flavoring. You had to grate the lemons yourself, which took forever, but the smell... oh, it was heavenly. It would fill her whole kitchen. She had this bright yellow wallpaper in her kitchen, with little white daisies on it. Very cheerful. Very 1970s. I think my cousin still has the old mixing bowl she used to use. It was one of those heavy ceramic ones, the kind you just can't find anymore unless you go to an antique store. I love antique stores, don't you? You never know what you're going to find. Just last week, I saw the most beautiful silver tea set in the window of that shop downtown, but the parking down there is just impossible... \n\nBut, what were we talking about? Oh, right. Being suspicious. No, before all this started with my sister's care, I just went about my business and assumed people were doing their jobs properly.\n\nDoctor: Outside of this recent situation, do you tend to mistrust or suspect other people in many parts of your life?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I'm not a paranoid person, if that's what you're getting at. I just go to work, do my job... I've been at the same company for fifteen years now. Fifteen years! You'd think they would appreciate that kind of loyalty. When I first started, the office manager was this wonderful woman named Barbara. Barbara had the most incredible collection of silk scarves. She wore a different one every day, tied in these elaborate knots. I asked her once how she did it, and she said she learned it from a magazine in the eighties. Vogue, maybe? Or Cosmopolitan. I used to read Cosmopolitan when I was in my twenties, but then it just got so... vulgar. The articles nowadays, I don't know who reads them. Young people, I suppose. My niece, she's twenty-two, and the things she posts on the internet... I don't understand it. Why would you want the whole world to know what you had for breakfast? She took a picture of an avocado toast last week. Avocado toast! It's just bread and a vegetable. Well, a fruit, technically. Avocados have large pits, so they're fruits. Like peaches. Oh, I love a good peach cobbler in the summer... \n\nBut no, to answer your question, I don't just walk around mistrusting everyone. It's only this specific thing with my sister's care because it's so obviously happening right in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you also find yourself mistrusting close friends or family members, or is it mainly this situation with your sister’s care and work?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say I mistrust my friends or family. Well, except maybe my cousin Elaine, but that's a whole different story. She borrowed a Tupperware container from me at Thanksgiving—the good kind, you know, the vintage ones with the starburst seal on the lid? They just don't make them like that anymore. Now it's all that flimsy plastic that warps in the dishwasher. Anyway, she swore she returned it, but I know for a fact she didn't. I've looked everywhere for it. I even checked the top shelf of the pantry, where I keep the holiday tins. I have quite a collection of those tins. Some of them are from the 1950s. There's one with a beautiful winter scene, little children ice skating on a pond... \n\nBut, what was I saying? Oh. No, my friends and family are fine. It's really just the people handling my sister's care. That's where the real problem is.\n\nDoctor: Outside of the situation with your sister’s care, do you find yourself suspicious of people in other parts of your life too?\n\nPatient: I feel like we keep circling back to this, Doctor. But no, I really don't. Like I said, I'm just a normal person going about my week. I go to work, I go to my Wednesday evening book club... I certainly don't sit there eyeing the other ladies suspiciously. Well, except for Brenda, but that's only because she always claims she read the book when she clearly just skimmed the Wikipedia summary. \n\nLast week we were supposed to read this wonderful historical fiction about a lighthouse keeper in the 1920s. I've always loved lighthouses. Did you know the oldest active lighthouse in the US is in New Jersey? Sandy Hook, I think it's called. My husband and I drove past it once on our way to a bed and breakfast in Cape May. Cape May is just gorgeous in the autumn. All those Victorian houses painted in those beautiful pastel colors. We stayed in a room that had a genuine clawfoot tub. I've always wanted a clawfoot tub in my own house, but the plumbing in our master bathroom just isn't set up for it. The contractor told us it would cost an absolute fortune to reroute the pipes, and with the price of copper these days... you wouldn't believe what they charge just for parts. \n\nAnyway... what was the question? Oh, right. No, I don't have a problem with people in general. I'm not a suspicious person. It's just the very real situation with my sister's care that I'm dealing with. Everything else in my life is perfectly fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you always tended to be suspicious of people and their motives, even before this recent situation started?\n\nPatient: I feel like you're asking me the exact same question in five different ways, Doctor. No, I haven't always been suspicious. I really don't go around looking for people's secret motives. \n\nAlthough, speaking of motives, my old neighbor Mr. Henderson always had an ulterior motive whenever he offered to sweep the shared walkway between our houses. I usually plant a few Cherokee Purples. They make a lovely caprese salad. I've never had a reason not to, until this business with my sister.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Doctor, I swear we are just going in circles here. I really don't know how else to say it—no. I haven't. I've never been a suspicious person. I'm starting to feel like you're not listening to me at all. 

It reminds me of when I tried to return a defective toaster oven to the department store last month. The customer service girl—she couldn't have been older than nineteen—just kept staring at her phone and repeating the same store policy at me like a parrot. I told her the heating element was completely shot, it wouldn't even melt cheese on a piece of toast. I only buy the thick-cut sourdough from the bakery downtown, the one with the green awning. They bake it fresh every morning at 4:00 AM. I know because my husband used to work the early shift at the post office and he'd smell the yeast in the air when he drove past. He worked there for thirty years, you know, before they offered him early retirement. The benefits package was quite good, actually, though the dental coverage left a lot to be desired. I ended up paying out of pocket for a crown last year. The dentist used that new ceramic material, it's supposed to match the natural color of your teeth perfectly. It's fascinating what they can do with dental technology these days, really... 

But honestly, no. I don't know why you keep asking me this. I've never had any reason to mistrust anyone before this whole mess with my sister's care.

If it's too acidic, the blooms turn blue, but I've always preferred the pink ones. I tried adding garden lime to the soil a few years ago to sweeten it, but I think I used a heavy hand because the leaves got all yellow and crispy at the edges. I bought the lime at that little independent garden center on Elm Street—the one that used to be an old service station back in the seventies. You can actually still see the faint outline in the concrete where the gas pumps used to be. They sell the most wonderful heirloom tomato seedlings there in the spring, though. I usually plant a few Cherokee Purples. They make a lovely caprese salad. Just some thick slices, fresh mozzarella, a little basil from my kitchen windowsill, and a good thick balsamic glaze... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, before all this started. No, I've always taken people at face value. I've never had a reason not to, until this business with my sister.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this kind of mistrust or suspicion about people in other situations earlier in life, even before your sister’s care issues started?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I swear we are just going in circles here. I really don't know how else to say it—no. I haven't. I've never been a suspicious person. I'm starting to feel like you're not listening to me at all. \n\nIt reminds me of when I tried to return a defective toaster oven to the department store last month. The customer service girl—she couldn't have been older than nineteen—just kept staring at her phone and repeating the same store policy at me like a parrot. I told her the heating element was completely shot, it wouldn't even melt cheese on a piece of toast. I only buy the thick-cut sourdough from the bakery downtown, the one with the green awning. They bake it fresh every morning at 4:00 AM. I know because my husband used to work the early shift at the post office and he'd smell the yeast in the air when he drove past. He worked there for thirty years, you know, before they offered him early retirement. The benefits package was quite good, actually, though the dental coverage left a lot to be desired. I ended up paying out of pocket for a crown last year. The dentist used that new ceramic material, it's supposed to match the natural color of your teeth perfectly. It's fascinating what they can do with dental technology these days, really... \n\nBut honestly, no. I don't know why you keep asking me this. I've never had a reason not to, until this business with my sister.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this kind of mistrust or suspicion about people in other situations earlier in life, even before your sister’s care issues started?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I swear we are just going in circles here. I really don't know how else to say it—no. I haven't. I've never been a suspicious person. I'm starting to feel like you're not listening to me at all. \n\nIt reminds me of when I tried to return a defective toaster oven to the department store last month. The customer service girl—she couldn't have been older than nineteen—just kept staring at her phone and repeating the same store policy at me like a parrot. I told her the heating element was completely shot, it wouldn't even melt cheese on a piece of toast. I only buy the thick-cut sourdough from the bakery downtown, the one with the green awning. They bake it fresh every morning at 4:00 AM. I know because my husband used to work the early shift at the post office and he'd smell the yeast in the air when he drove past. He worked there for thirty years, you know, before they offered him early retirement. The benefits package was quite good, actually, though the dental coverage left a lot to be desired. I ended up paying out of pocket for a crown last year. The dentist used that new ceramic material, it's supposed to match the natural color of your teeth perfectly. It's fascinating what they can do with dental technology these days, really... \n\nBut honestly, no. I don't know why you keep asking me this. I've never had any reason to mistrust anyone before this whole mess with my sister's care.\n\n[Last Question]\nBefore the last 11 months, would you have described yourself as someone who preferred to spend most of your time alone?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 59 / first_line_supervisor_of_office_or_administrative_support_worker / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, not at all. I've always enjoyed being around people. I mean, I've worked in offices my whole life, you can't exactly be a hermit when you're managing an administrative team. 

I used to organize the annual summer picnic for my department. We'd always go to Centennial Park, the one with the big gazebo. I'd make my famous potato salad. The secret is adding just a splash of pickle juice to the mayonnaise before you mix it in. My grandmother taught me that. She was a wonderful cook, though she never wrote anything down, which was so frustrating. She made this incredible peach cobbler every August when the local orchards were ready for picking. We used to drive out to Miller's Farm—it's been family-owned for four generations, you know. They have a little shop where they sell homemade jams and those lovely beeswax candles. I bought a lavender one last time I was there, it smells heavenly when you burn it in the evenings. It's supposed to help you sleep, which is good because I've always been a bit of a light sleeper. Even a car driving by too fast can wake me up. 

But no, I've never been one to just sit alone all day. I've always had a very full, active life.

I got my degree years ago and we actually worked. We didn't have smartphones to distract us, just proper filing and respect. They want to push me out of making decisions. \n\nIt's just like when my husband and I bought our first house. But this specific situation, with the appointments and the information being hidden, that started about 11 months ago. Right around the time my sister's health took a sudden turn for the worse and I had to step in to coordinate all her care. \n\nShe has this lovely little house over in the historic district, you know, the ones with the big wrap-around porches? We used to sit out there in the evenings with a glass of iced tea. She grew mint in these terracotta pots on the steps. Real mint, not that peppermint extract nonsense they put in everything now. It smelled so fresh, especially after a summer rain. We'd just sit and watch the fireflies. You don't see fireflies like you used to, do you? I think it's all the pesticides people spray on their lawns. Everyone wants that perfect, unnatural green grass. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he's out there every Saturday morning at 7 AM with his lawnmower and his chemicals. It's ridiculous. I told him once, I said, \"You're poisoning the earth, Arthur.\" He just ignored me, of course. Typical. \n\nBut yes, 11 months ago. That's when I had to start dealing with all these doctors and insurance people for her, and that's when I realized how they operate. How they try to keep you in the dark.\n\nDoctor: Before these last 11 months, had you generally been suspicious of other people for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say I've always been suspicious. Like I said, I used to be quite trusting. Almost naive, really. I remember when I first started working as an administrative supervisor—oh, this must have been thirty years ago now. I was so eager to please everyone. I used to bring in homemade baked goods every Friday. Muffins, mostly. Blueberry, bran, sometimes a nice lemon poppyseed. I had this wonderful recipe for lemon poppyseed muffins from my grandmother. She used real lemon zest, not that artificial flavoring. You had to grate the lemons yourself, which took forever, but the smell... oh, it was heavenly. It would fill her whole kitchen. She had this bright yellow wallpaper in her kitchen, with little white daisies on it. Very cheerful. Very 1970s. I think my cousin still has the old mixing bowl she used to use. It was one of those heavy ceramic ones, the kind you just can't find anymore unless you go to an antique store. I love antique stores, don't you? You never know what you're going to find. Just last week, I saw the most beautiful silver tea set in the window of that shop downtown, but the parking down there is just impossible... \n\nBut, what were we talking about? Oh, right. Being suspicious. No, before all this started with my sister's care, I just went about my business and assumed people were doing their jobs properly.\n\nDoctor: Outside of this recent situation, do you tend to mistrust or suspect other people in many parts of your life?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I'm not a paranoid person, if that's what you're getting at. I just go to work, do my job... I've been at the same company for fifteen years now. Fifteen years! You'd think they would appreciate that kind of loyalty. When I first started, the office manager was this wonderful woman named Barbara. Barbara had the most incredible collection of silk scarves. She wore a different one every day, tied in these elaborate knots. I asked her once how she did it, and she said she learned it from a magazine in the eighties. Vogue, maybe? Or Cosmopolitan. I used to read Cosmopolitan when I was in my twenties, but then it just got so... vulgar. The articles nowadays, I don't know who reads them. Young people, I suppose. My niece, she's twenty-two, and the things she posts on the internet... I don't understand it. Why would you want the whole world to know what you had for breakfast? She took a picture of an avocado toast last week. Avocado toast! It's just bread and a vegetable. Well, a fruit, technically. Avocados have large pits, so they're fruits. Like peaches. Oh, I love a good peach cobbler in the summer... \n\nBut no, to answer your question, I don't just walk around mistrusting everyone. It's only this specific thing with my sister's care because it's so obviously happening right in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you also find yourself mistrusting close friends or family members, or is it mainly this situation with your sister’s care and work?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say I mistrust my friends or family. Well, except maybe my cousin Elaine, but that's a whole different story. She borrowed a Tupperware container from me at Thanksgiving—the good kind, you know, the vintage ones with the starburst seal on the lid? They just don't make them like that anymore. Now it's all that flimsy plastic that warps in the dishwasher. Anyway, she swore she returned it, but I know for a fact she didn't. I've looked everywhere for it. I even checked the top shelf of the pantry, where I keep the holiday tins. I have quite a collection of those tins. Some of them are from the 1950s. There's one with a beautiful winter scene, little children ice skating on a pond... \n\nBut, what was I saying? Oh. No, my friends and family are fine. It's really just the people handling my sister's care. That's where the real problem is.\n\nDoctor: Outside of the situation with your sister’s care, do you find yourself suspicious of people in other parts of your life too?\n\nPatient: I feel like we keep circling back to this, Doctor. But no, I really don't. Like I said, I'm just a normal person going about my week. I go to work, I go to my Wednesday evening book club... I certainly don't sit there eyeing the other ladies suspiciously. Well, except for Brenda, but that's only because she always claims she read the book when she clearly just skimmed the Wikipedia summary. \n\nLast week we were supposed to read this wonderful historical fiction about a lighthouse keeper in the 1920s. I've always loved lighthouses. Did you know the oldest active lighthouse in the US is in New Jersey? Sandy Hook, I think it's called. My husband and I drove past it once on our way to a bed and breakfast in Cape May. Cape May is just gorgeous in the autumn. All those Victorian houses painted in those beautiful pastel colors. We stayed in a room that had a genuine clawfoot tub. I've always wanted a clawfoot tub in my own house, but the plumbing in our master bathroom just isn't set up for it. The contractor told us it would cost an absolute fortune to reroute the pipes, and with the price of copper these days... you wouldn't believe what they charge just for parts. \n\nAnyway... what was the question? Oh, right. No, I don't have a problem with people in general. I'm not a suspicious person. It's just the very real situation with my sister's care that I'm dealing with. Everything else in my life is perfectly fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you always tended to be suspicious of people and their motives, even before this recent situation started?\n\nPatient: I feel like you're asking me the exact same question in five different ways, Doctor. No, I haven't always been suspicious. I really don't go around looking for people's secret motives. \n\nAlthough, speaking of motives, my old neighbor Mr. Henderson always had an ulterior motive whenever he offered to sweep the shared walkway between our houses. He really just wanted an excuse to complain about my hydrangeas. Hydrangeas are incredibly temperamental, you know. You have to get the soil pH absolutely perfect. If it's too acidic, the blooms turn blue, but I've always preferred the pink ones. I tried adding garden lime to the soil a few years ago to sweeten it, but I think I used a heavy hand because the leaves got all yellow and crispy at the edges. I bought the lime at that little independent garden center on Elm Street—the one that used to be an old service station back in the seventies. You can actually still see the faint outline in the concrete where the gas pumps used to be. They sell the most wonderful heirloom tomato seedlings there in the spring, though. I usually plant a few Cherokee Purples. They make a lovely caprese salad. Just some thick slices, fresh mozzarella, a little basil from my kitchen windowsill, and a good thick balsamic glaze... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, before all this started. No, I've always taken people at face value. I've never had a reason not to, until this business with my sister.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this kind of mistrust or suspicion about people in other situations earlier in life, even before your sister’s care issues started?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I swear we are just going in circles here. I really don't know how else to say it—no. I haven't. I've never been a suspicious person. I'm starting to feel like you're not listening to me at all. \n\nIt reminds me of when I tried to return a defective toaster oven to the department store last month. The customer service girl—she couldn't have been older than nineteen—just kept staring at her phone and repeating the same store policy at me like a parrot. I told her the heating element was completely shot, it wouldn't even melt cheese on a piece of toast. I only buy the thick-cut sourdough from the bakery downtown, the one with the green awning. They bake it fresh every morning at 4:00 AM. I know because my husband used to work the early shift at the post office and he'd smell the yeast in the air when he drove past. I've never had any reason to mistrust anyone before this whole mess with my sister's care.\n\nDoctor: Before the last 11 months, would you have described yourself as someone who preferred to spend most of your time alone?\n\nPatient: No, not at all.
