She'd say she forgot to transfer the calls, but I know she was just busy planning her wedding on company time. I told her, you know, having a bachelor's degree doesn't teach you work ethic. I got my degree years ago and we actually worked. We didn't have smartphones to distract us, just proper filing and respect. I had this beautiful oak desk back then, and my supervisor at the time, Mr. Henderson, he always said I had the sharpest eye for detail in the whole department... \n\nWait, what was I saying? Oh, right. The appointments. Everyone keeps telling me it’s just mistakes, just administrative errors. But I know better. I know they’re deliberately keeping information from me and trying to push me out of decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe these people are deliberately trying to harm or persecute you?\n\nPatient: Harm me? Well... I don't know if I'd say *harm*, like physical harm, but they are certainly trying to push me out, to make me look incompetent. Just like that girl at work I mentioned. It's all about making me look bad so they can take over. Which reminds me, my sister's neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she had something similar happen. She was on the HOA board, and they just slowly stopped sending her the meeting minutes. Claimed it was a \"glitch\" in the email system. Can you believe that? A glitch. I told my sister, I said, \"Martha, you watch your back around that HOA.\" Martha and I used to go to these lovely estate sales on the weekends before all this started. We found this gorgeous vintage lamp once, solid brass, just incredible craftsmanship. You don't see that anymore. Everything is cheap plastic now. Just like the people they hire. No substance. But anyway... what did you ask me? Oh, right, persecute. I wouldn't use that word, no. I just know they're hiding things from me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard or seen things that other people around you could not?\n\nPatient: Heard or seen things? Like ghosts or something? No, Doctor, I'm not crazy. I don't see things that aren't there. My vision is perfectly fine, actually. I just had my eyes checked last month at that new optometrist over on 4th Street. Dr. Evans, I think his name was. Very young, maybe too young, honestly. He had this whole high-tech machine that puffs air into your eye—I hate that thing. When I was a girl, Dr. Miller just used a simple light and a chart. He was such a wonderful man, Dr. Miller. He used to give out these little butterscotch candies after your appointment. You know, the ones wrapped in the yellow cellophane? I haven't seen those in years. I tried looking for them at the grocery store for Halloween, but all they have now is that sour gummy nonsense. Kids today don't appreciate a good hard candy. They just want instant gratification. It's the same with the young staff at my office, like I was saying before. No patience. No attention to detail. \n\nBut no, to answer your question, I don't hear voices or see things. I just see what's actually happening right in front of me, even if everyone else wants to pretend it's not.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there is any chance you might be mistaken about what these people are doing?\n\nPatient: Mistaken? No, Doctor, I'm not mistaken. I know what I'm seeing. Like I said when I first sat down—the appointments moving, the wrong bills, the \"forgotten\" phone calls... it's a pattern. Everyone keeps trying to tell me it's just administrative errors, just mistakes. But I know they're deliberately keeping information from me. They want to push me out of making decisions. \n\nIt's just like when my husband and I bought our first house. The real estate agent, she tried to tell us the plumbing issues were just \"routine maintenance.\" Routine! We had to rip out the entire guest bathroom. It was a nightmare. We had this lovely floral wallpaper in there, hydrangeas, I think. Or maybe peonies. I always get those two mixed up. My mother had the most beautiful peony bush in her front yard. Every spring, it would just explode with pink. She used to cut them and put them in this crystal vase she got for her wedding. Real crystal, not that cheap glass they sell now. It caught the light so beautifully in the afternoons... \n\nBut anyway, no. I'm not mistaken about this. I know what they're doing. They can call it mistakes all they want, but I see right through it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been this suspicious of other people for as long as you can remember, or did this only start recently?\n\nPatient: Recently? Well, I suppose it depends on what you mean by recently. But no, I haven't always been like this. I used to be very trusting, probably too trusting if you ask me. But this specific situation, with the appointments and the information being hidden, that started about 11 months ago. Right around the time my sister's health took a sudden turn for the worse and I had to step in to coordinate all her care. \n\nShe has this lovely little house over in the historic district, you know, the ones with the big wrap-around porches? We used to sit out there in the evenings with a glass of iced tea. She grew mint in these terracotta pots on the steps. Real mint, not that peppermint extract nonsense they put in everything now. It smelled so fresh, especially after a summer rain. We'd just sit and watch the fireflies. You don't see fireflies like you used to, do you? I think it's all the pesticides people spray on their lawns. Everyone wants that perfect, unnatural green grass. Like I said, I used to be quite trusting. Almost naive, really. I remember when I first started working as an administrative supervisor—oh, this must have been thirty years ago now. I was so eager to please everyone. I used to bring in homemade baked goods every Friday. Muffins, mostly. Blueberry, bran, sometimes a nice lemon poppyseed. I had this wonderful recipe for lemon poppyseed muffins from my grandmother. She used real lemon zest, not that artificial flavoring. You had to grate the lemons yourself, which took forever, but the smell... oh, it was heavenly. It would fill her whole kitchen. She had this bright yellow wallpaper in her kitchen, with little white daisies on it. Very cheerful. Very 1970s. I think my cousin still has the old mixing bowl she used to use. It was one of those heavy ceramic ones, the kind you just can't find anymore unless you go to an antique store. I love antique stores, don't you? You never know what you're going to find. Just last week, I saw the most beautiful silver tea set in the window of that shop downtown, but the parking down there is just impossible... \n\nBut, what were we talking about? Oh, right. Being suspicious. You'd think they would appreciate that kind of loyalty. When I first started, the office manager was this wonderful woman named Barbara. Barbara had the most incredible collection of silk scarves. She wore a different one every day, tied in these elaborate knots. I asked her once how she did it, and she said she learned it from a magazine in the eighties. Vogue, maybe? Or Cosmopolitan. My niece, she's twenty-two, and the things she posts on the internet... I don't understand it. Why would you want the whole world to know what you had for breakfast? Kids today don't appreciate a good hard candy. They just want instant gratification. It's the same with the young staff at my office, like I was saying before. No patience. No attention to detail. \n\nBut no, to answer your question, I don't hear voices or see things. I just see what's actually happening right in front of me, even if everyone else wants to pretend it's not.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there is any chance you might be mistaken about what these people are doing?\n\nPatient: Mistaken? No, Doctor, I'm not mistaken. I know what I'm seeing. Like I said when I first sat down—the appointments moving, the wrong bills, the \"forgotten\" phone calls... it's a pattern. Everyone keeps trying to tell me it's just administrative errors, just mistakes. But I know they're deliberately keeping information from me. They want to push me out of making decisions. \n\nIt's just like when my husband and I bought our first house. The real estate agent, she tried to tell us the plumbing issues were just \"routine maintenance.\" Routine! We had to rip out the entire guest bathroom. It was a nightmare. We had this lovely floral wallpaper in there, hydrangeas, I think. Or maybe peonies. I always get those two mixed up. My mother had the most beautiful peony bush in her front yard. Every spring, it would just explode with pink. She used to cut them and put them in this crystal vase she got for her wedding. Real crystal, not that cheap glass they sell now. It caught the light so beautifully in the afternoons... \n\nBut anyway, no. I'm not mistaken about this. I know what they're doing. They can call it mistakes all they want, but I see right through it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been this suspicious of other people for as long as you can remember, or did this only start recently?\n\nPatient: Recently? Well, I suppose it depends on what you mean by recently. But no, I haven't always been like this. I used to be very trusting, probably too trusting if you ask me. But this specific situation, with the appointments and the information being hidden, that started about 11 months ago. Right around the time my sister's health took a sudden turn for the worse and I had to step in to coordinate all her care. \n\nShe has this lovely little house over in the historic district, you know, the ones with the big wrap-around porches? We used to sit out there in the evenings with a glass of iced tea. She grew mint in these terracotta pots on the steps. Real mint, not that peppermint extract nonsense they put in everything now. It smelled so fresh, especially after a summer rain. We'd just sit and watch the fireflies. You don't see fireflies like you used to, do you? I think it's all the pesticides people spray on their lawns. Everyone wants that perfect, unnatural green grass. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he's out there every Saturday morning at 7 AM with his lawnmower and his chemicals. It's ridiculous. I told him once, I said, \"You're poisoning the earth, Arthur.\" He just ignored me, of course. Typical. \n\nBut yes, 11 months ago. That's when I had to start dealing with all these doctors and insurance people for her, and that's when I realized how they operate. How they try to keep you in the dark.\n\nDoctor: Before these last 11 months, had you generally been suspicious of other people for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say I've always been suspicious. Like I said, I used to be quite trusting. Almost naive, really. I remember when I first started working as an administrative supervisor—oh, this must have been thirty years ago now. I was so eager to please everyone. I used to bring in homemade baked goods every Friday. Muffins, mostly. Blueberry, bran, sometimes a nice lemon poppyseed. I had this wonderful recipe for lemon poppyseed muffins from my grandmother. She used real lemon zest, not that artificial flavoring. You had to grate the lemons yourself, which took forever, but the smell... oh, it was heavenly. It would fill her whole kitchen. She had this bright yellow wallpaper in her kitchen, with little white daisies on it. Very cheerful. Very 1970s. I think my cousin still has the old mixing bowl she used to use. It was one of those heavy ceramic ones, the kind you just can't find anymore unless you go to an antique store. I love antique stores, don't you? You never know what you're going to find. Just last week, I saw the most beautiful silver tea set in the window of that shop downtown, but the parking down there is just impossible... \n\nBut, what were we talking about? Oh, right. Being suspicious. No, before all this started with my sister's care, I just went about my business and assumed people were doing their jobs properly.\n\nDoctor: Outside of this recent situation, do you tend to mistrust or suspect other people in many parts of your life?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I'm not a paranoid person, if that's what you're getting at. I just go to work, do my job... I've been at the same company for fifteen years now. Fifteen years! You'd think they would appreciate that kind of loyalty. When I first started, the office manager was this wonderful woman named Barbara. Barbara had the most incredible collection of silk scarves. She wore a different one every day, tied in these elaborate knots. I asked her once how she did it, and she said she learned it from a magazine in the eighties. Vogue, maybe? Or Cosmopolitan. I used to read Cosmopolitan when I was in my twenties, but then it just got so... vulgar. The articles nowadays, I don't know who reads them. Young people, I suppose. My niece, she's twenty-two, and the things she posts on the internet... I don't understand it. Why would you want the whole world to know what you had for breakfast? She took a picture of an avocado toast last week. Avocado toast! It's just bread and a vegetable. Well, a fruit, technically. Avocados have large pits, so they're fruits. Like peaches. Oh, I love a good peach cobbler in the summer... \n\nBut no, to answer your question, I don't just walk around mistrusting everyone. It's only this specific thing with my sister's care because it's so obviously happening right in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you also find yourself mistrusting close friends or family members, or is it mainly this situation with your sister’s care and work?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say I mistrust my friends or family. Well, except maybe my cousin Elaine, but that's a whole different story. She borrowed a Tupperware container from me at Thanksgiving—the good kind, you know, the vintage ones with the starburst seal on the lid? They just don't make them like that anymore. Now it's all that flimsy plastic that warps in the dishwasher. Anyway, she swore she returned it, but I know for a fact she didn't. I've looked everywhere for it. I even checked the top shelf of the pantry, where I keep the holiday tins. I have quite a collection of those tins. Some of them are from the 1950s. There's one with a beautiful winter scene, little children ice skating on a pond... \n\nBut, what was I saying? Oh. No, my friends and family are fine. It's really just the people handling my sister's care. That's where the real problem is.\n\nDoctor: Outside of the situation with your sister’s care, do you find yourself suspicious of people in other parts of your life too?\n\nPatient: I feel like we keep circling back to this, Doctor. But no, I really don't. Like I said, I'm just a normal person going about my week. I go to work, I go to my Wednesday evening book club... I certainly don't sit there eyeing the other ladies suspiciously. Well, except for Brenda, but that's only because she always claims she read the book when she clearly just skimmed the Wikipedia summary. \n\nLast week we were supposed to read this wonderful historical fiction about a lighthouse keeper in the 1920s. I've always loved lighthouses. Did you know the oldest active lighthouse in the US is in New Jersey? Sandy Hook, I think it's called. My husband and I drove past it once on our way to a bed and breakfast in Cape May. Cape May is just gorgeous in the autumn. All those Victorian houses painted in those beautiful pastel colors. We stayed in a room that had a genuine clawfoot tub. I've always wanted a clawfoot tub in my own house, but the plumbing in our master bathroom just isn't set up for it. The contractor told us it would cost an absolute fortune to reroute the pipes, and with the price of copper these days... you wouldn't believe what they charge just for parts. \n\nAnyway... what was the question? Oh, right. No, I don't have a problem with people in general. I'm not a suspicious person. It's just the very real situation with my sister's care that I'm dealing with. Everything else in my life is perfectly fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you always tended to be suspicious of people and their motives, even before this recent situation started?\n\nPatient: I feel like you're asking me the exact same question in five different ways, Doctor. No, I haven't always been suspicious. I really don't go around looking for people's secret motives. \n\nAlthough, speaking of motives, my old neighbor Mr. Henderson always had an ulterior motive whenever he offered to sweep the shared walkway between our houses. He really just wanted an excuse to complain about my hydrangeas. Hydrangeas are incredibly temperamental, you know. You have to get the soil pH absolutely perfect. If it's too acidic, the blooms turn blue, but I've always preferred the pink ones. I tried adding garden lime to the soil a few years ago to sweeten it, but I think I used a heavy hand because the leaves got all yellow and crispy at the edges. I bought the lime at that little independent garden center on Elm Street—the one that used to be an old service station back in the seventies. You can actually still see the faint outline in the concrete where the gas pumps used to be. They sell the most wonderful heirloom tomato seedlings there in the spring, though. I usually plant a few Cherokee Purples. They make a lovely caprese salad. Just some thick slices, fresh mozzarella, a little basil from my kitchen windowsill, and a good thick balsamic glaze... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, before all this started. No, I've always taken people at face value. I've never had a reason not to, until this business with my sister.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this kind of mistrust or suspicion about people in other situations earlier in life, even before your sister’s care issues started?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I swear we are just going in circles here. I really don't know how else to say it—no. I haven't. I've never been a suspicious person. I'm starting to feel like you're not listening to me at all. \n\nIt reminds me of when I tried to return a defective toaster oven to the department store last month. The customer service girl—she couldn't have been older than nineteen—just kept staring at her phone and repeating the same store policy at me like a parrot. I told her the heating element was completely shot, it wouldn't even melt cheese on a piece of toast. I only buy the thick-cut sourdough from the bakery downtown, the one with the green awning. They bake it fresh every morning at 4:00 AM. I know because my husband used to work the early shift at the post office and he'd smell the yeast in the air when he drove past. He worked there for thirty years, you know, before they offered him early retirement. The benefits package was quite good, actually, though the dental coverage left a lot to be desired. I ended up paying out of pocket for a crown last year. The dentist used that new ceramic material, it's supposed to match the natural color of your teeth perfectly. It's fascinating what they can do with dental technology these days, really... \n\nBut honestly, no. I don't know why you keep asking me this. I've never had any reason to mistrust anyone before this whole mess with my sister's care.\n\nDoctor: Before the last 11 months, would you have described yourself as someone who preferred to spend most of your time alone?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. I've always enjoyed being around people. I mean, I've worked in offices my whole life, you can't exactly be a hermit when you're managing an administrative team. \n\nI used to organize the annual summer picnic for my department. We'd always go to Centennial Park, the one with the big gazebo. I'd make my famous potato salad. The secret is adding just a splash of pickle juice to the mayonnaise before you mix it in. My grandmother taught me that. She was a wonderful cook, though she never wrote anything down, which was so frustrating. She made this incredible peach cobbler every August when the local orchards were ready for picking. We used to drive out to Miller's Farm—it's been family-owned for four generations, you know. They have a little shop where they sell homemade jams and those lovely beeswax candles. I bought a lavender one last time I was there, it smells heavenly when you burn it in the evenings. It's supposed to help you sleep, which is good because I've always been a bit of a light sleeper. Even a car driving by too fast can wake me up. \n\nBut no, I've never been one to just sit alone all day. I've always had a very full, active life.\n\nDoctor: Before this recent situation started, did you usually assume other people had bad motives or might try to use you?\n\nPatient: Doctor, are you writing down my answers at all? Because I feel like I'm taking a test where every question is just a trick version of the last one. No, I didn't assume people were trying to use me. \n\nI mean, unless you count Susan in the payroll department always wandering off with my good gel pens. I buy those fine-point ones out of my own pocket, the 0.5 millimeter tips, because I have very small handwriting and the standard ones just make my signature look like a smudge on the page. I have to get them from this specific stationary shop downtown because the corporate office supply catalog only carries the medium points. The man who runs that shop, Arthur, he’s been there since the late eighties. He actually just had his first great-grandchild, a little girl. He showed me pictures on his phone when I was in there last week. They named her Eleanor, which I think is just so elegant. It's really refreshing to see traditional names coming back, don't you think? Everyone is naming their children such strange things lately, like they're trying to invent new words. My cousin's daughter named her baby 'Kyler' and I just... I don't understand it. It sounds like a typo. But she did have a beautiful nursery set up for him, all painted in this very soothing sage green with little woodland creatures stenciled on the baseboards... \n\nWait, I'm sorry, I completely lost my train of thought. But no, to answer your question, I never walked around thinking people were out to get me or use me. Not until all this happened.\n\nDoctor: Before age 18 or in your early twenties, did you already tend to be very suspicious of other people?\n\nPatient: Are you serious right now? We really are just going around and around in circles. I don't know how many different ways I can say the exact same thing to you. No, I wasn't suspicious of people when I was young. I was perfectly normal. \n\nI was actually a cheerleader in high school, if you can believe it. We had these awful, scratchy wool uniforms. The colors were maroon and gold, which really washes out my complexion, but my mother insisted I stick with it because it would look good on college applications. She was always so worried about college. She used to clip articles out of the Sunday paper about different universities and leave them on my pillow. I still read the Sunday paper, actually, mostly for the crosswords. I do them in pen. My husband used to tease me about it, he said I was too confident. But I just hate the way pencil lead smudges on the newsprint. It gets all over the side of your hand and then you transfer it to the kitchen counter. We have those nice quartz countertops, we put them in about five years ago when we remodeled the kitchen. The contractor was an absolute nightmare, though. He kept delaying the project and leaving his tools everywhere. I finally had to threaten to withhold his final payment before he actually finished the backsplash. It's a subway tile, very classic, it never goes out of style. I think trendy kitchens look so dated after just a few years. Like those avocado green appliances everyone had in the seventies... \n\nAnyway, I don't know why you're so fixated on my childhood. I was perfectly fine back then. I wasn't suspicious of anyone.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered that these problems with your sister’s care might not actually be deliberate, even a little bit?\n\nPatient: No, absolutely not. I mean, how could they just be mistakes? I’m here because for months now I’ve been seeing the exact same pattern. Her appointments get moved without anyone telling me, the bills show up completely wrong, and these people just conveniently \"forget\" to call me back. \n\nIt reminds me of when I tried to cancel my cable service last year. You know how they do that? They keep transferring you to different departments, hoping you'll just give up and keep paying. I was on the phone for two and a half hours one Tuesday afternoon. I remember it was a Tuesday because it was raining so hard the gutters overflowed. We had to get someone out to clean them the next day. The man who came, Ricky, he was so polite. He even cleaned the skylight over the sunroom while he was up there. I love that sunroom. I have a whole collection of African violets in there. They're incredibly finicky, you know. You can't get water on their leaves or they spot, and they need just the right amount of indirect light. I have this beautiful one with ruffled pink edges that I got at a plant swap at the community center. They hold those every spring. They used to have a bake sale with it, but they stopped after the new health department regulations... \n\nBut anyway, no. Even when everyone keeps telling me it’s just clerical errors or whatever excuse they come up with, I know what I'm seeing. They’re deliberately keeping information from me. They are actively trying to push me out of making decisions for her, and it's not an accident.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had trouble trusting people in situations unrelated to your sister’s care?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. I've always been a very trusting person, maybe even too trusting sometimes. I mean, I supervise a whole team of administrative staff, and you have to trust them to do their jobs, right? You can't micromanage everything. \n\nLike with the new filing system we implemented last quarter. I let Brenda handle the color-coding, which was a big leap for me because I have very specific ideas about organization. I usually prefer a chronological system with alphabetical sub-folders, but she wanted to do it by department. She's been with the company for three years now. She brought in these amazing homemade blueberry muffins for her work anniversary. She said the secret is sour cream in the batter, which I never would have thought of. I usually use buttermilk when I bake. My grandmother taught me how to make biscuits from scratch when I was just a little girl, standing on a stepstool in her kitchen in Ohio. She had this beautiful old farmhouse sink, the deep porcelain kind that everyone pays a fortune for nowadays. I saw one just like it on one of those home renovation shows last week. The hosts are this married couple, they're always bickering about the budget, but they do incredible work. They took this completely dilapidated barn and turned it into a guest house... \n\nBut anyway, no, I don't have trouble trusting people in general. It's just this specific situation with my sister's care because it's so incredibly obvious what they're doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any beliefs that others would consider very strange or impossible, even if they are not related to your sister’s care?\n\nPatient: Strange beliefs? No, of course not. I don't believe in ghosts or aliens or anything silly like that, if that's what you mean. I'm a very practical person. I mean, I drive a silver Honda Accord, for heaven's sake. You really can't get much more grounded than that. It gets excellent gas mileage, too. I've had it for six years and it's never given me a bit of trouble, except for that one time the check engine light came on last winter. \n\nIt turned out to just be a loose gas cap. The mechanic at the dealership was so nice about it, he didn't even charge me for the diagnostic test. His name was Dave. Or maybe Dan? He had this very distinct mustache, kind of like Tom Selleck. I used to love watching Magnum P.I. back in the eighties. The scenery in Hawaii was just breathtaking. My husband and I actually went to Maui for our twentieth anniversary. We stayed at this lovely resort where they left fresh orchids on the pillows every single night. The humidity did absolute murder to my hair, though. I have naturally wavy hair, and the minute the humidity hits seventy percent, I just puff up like a poodle. I tried all sorts of expensive anti-frizz serums from the drugstore, but nothing really worked until I found this argan oil at a little boutique salon downtown. The owner imports it directly from Morocco. She goes there twice a year to buy it in bulk, and she brings back these gorgeous hand-painted ceramic bowls, too. I bought one to keep my keys in by the front door. \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right. No, I don't have any impossible beliefs. I'm very realistic.\n\nDoctor: Before these past 11 months, did you usually find it hard to trust other people in close relationships?\n\nPatient: No, like I said before, I've always been pretty trusting. I've been married for 32 years, you know, and you can't make a marriage work that long without trust. My husband, Richard, he's a retired accountant. We actually met at a tax preparation seminar back in 1988. I know, it sounds incredibly boring, but we ended up sitting next to each other at lunch because they ran out of the turkey sandwiches and we both had to settle for the egg salad. \n\nI actually make a fantastic egg salad now. The trick is a little bit of Dijon mustard and some finely chopped celery for crunch. I usually get my celery from the farmer's market on Saturdays. There's this one vendor, the Miller family, they have the crispest vegetables. They also sell these wonderful homemade jams. Last week I bought a jar of their peach preserves. I put it on my toast every morning with my coffee. I have to have my coffee, just one cup, but it has to be hazelnut. I buy the beans whole and grind them myself. It really makes a difference in the flavor, don't you think? My sister used to just buy that instant stuff in the jar... \n\nBut anyway, no, I haven't ever had trouble trusting people close to me. That's never been an issue.\n\nDoctor: Before age 18 or in your early twenties, did you already tend to be very suspicious of other people?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. When I was younger, I was actually very open. Almost naive, really. I remember when I went off to college—I went to Ohio State, which was a huge adjustment from our little town. I shared a dorm room with a girl named Sarah. She was from Chicago and she just seemed so sophisticated. She had this incredible collection of vintage scarves. She'd tie them in her hair or around her neck, and she always looked like she just stepped out of a fashion magazine. \n\nI tried to copy her look once, but I just ended up looking like I was trying to hide a bad haircut. Which, actually, I did have a terrible haircut my sophomore year. I let a friend from my biology study group practice on me because she wanted to go to cosmetology school. She ended up cutting my bangs so short they stood straight up for a month. I had to wear headbands every single day. I bought this one velvet headband, a deep burgundy color, from a little boutique near campus. The woman who owned the shop had the most beautiful Persian cat that would just sleep on the glass display counter all day. I've always wanted a cat, but Richard is terribly allergic. His eyes get all red and puffy if he's even in the same room as a cat. We got a golden retriever instead, Buster. He passed away a few years ago, but he was the sweetest dog. He used to carry his leash in his mouth when it was time for a walk...\n\nBut, no, to answer your question, I was never suspicious of people back then. I just assumed everyone meant well."
